
======================================================
VLM frame 0 (window 0-0)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

==============================================================

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio
Surveillance cameras (1) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Main Store Security Camera: The camera monitors the cashier desk, the main entrance and exit door, the firearm display showcases, and the main shopping area inside the store. It provides a wide-angle view of customer movement, employee activity, weapon handling interactions, and behavior near secured weapon display cases.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Customer interacts with display case, potentially handling merchandise",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject leans over glass display case",
    "person reaches into or interacts with case contents",
    "second individual walks through the center of the room"
  ],
  "concern": "low"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.03,
  "label": "normal",
  "reason": "Customer interacts with display case, potentially handling merchandise",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject leans over glass display case",
    "person reaches into or interacts with case contents",
    "second individual walks through the center of the room"
  ]
}

======================================================
VLM frame 300 (window 180-300)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio
Surveillance cameras (1) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Main Store Security Camera: The camera monitors the cashier desk, the main entrance and exit door, the firearm display showcases, and the main shopping area inside the store. It provides a wide-angle view of customer movement, employee activity, weapon handling interactions, and behavior near secured weapon display cases.
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 240) VLM observation: An individual is standing in the center of a gun store, appearing to handle or inspect an object held in their hands. The store displays numerous firearms on the walls and within glass counters. Weapon note: The person is holding an object that appears to be a firearm.. Flags — gun: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 300) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a retail store, with one person leaning over a display case while another person is positioned behind the counter area. The individual leaning over the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: unclear; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 360) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively smashing and reaching into glass display cases inside a gun store. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and appear to be looting merchandise from the cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 420) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into glass display cases inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing that obscures their identities. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 540) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are actively reaching into and over the glass display cases in a gun store. Both individuals appear to be searching or grabbing items from the cases while the store is otherwise empty. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 600) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are leaning over and reaching into the glass display cases of a gun store. The individuals appear to be actively removing or searching through the merchandise inside the cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 660) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into glass display cases inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-coverage clothing and appear to be concealing their faces. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.

VLM frame 720 (window 600-720)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.

======================================================
VLM frame 900 (window 780-900)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —

from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal

lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio
Surveillance cameras (1) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Main Store Security Camera: The camera monitors the cashier desk, the main entrance and exit door, the firearm display showcases, and the main shopping area inside the store. It provides a wide-angle view of customer movement, employee activity, weapon handling interactions, and behavior near secured weapon display cases.
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 300) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a retail store, with one person leaning over a display case while another person is positioned behind the counter area. The individual leaning over the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: unclear; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 360) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively smashing and reaching into glass display cases inside a gun store. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and appear to be looting merchandise from the cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 420) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into glass display cases inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing that obscures their identities. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 660) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into glass display cases inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-coverage clothing and appear to be concealing their faces. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 720) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively looting a display case inside a gun store, with one person reaching into the case while the other stands nearby. The store walls are lined with numerous firearms on display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 780) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively reaching into and over a glass display case inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-body clothing and are positioned behind the counter area. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 840) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively smashing and reaching into a glass display case inside a retail store. The individuals appear to be looting the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 900) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into a glass display case inside a retail store, appearing to remove merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing while positioned behind the counter area. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1020) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively reaching into and clearing out the contents of a glass display case inside a retail store. Both individuals are leaning over the counter, appearing to remove merchandise from the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 960) VLM observation: An individual is leaning over a glass display case in a gun store, appearing to reach into the interior of the case. The person is dressed in dark clothing and is positioned behind the counter area. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1020) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively reaching into and clearing out the contents of a glass display case inside a retail store. Both individuals are leaning over the counter, appearing to remove merchandise from the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1080) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over a display counter in a gun store, with one person appearing to reach into the case while the other stands nearby. The store displays numerous firearms on the walls and in glass cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

======================================================
VLM frame 1260 (window 1140-1260)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio
Surveillance cameras (1) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Main Store Security Camera: The camera monitors the cashier desk, the main entrance and exit door, the firearm display showcases, and the main shopping area inside the store. It provides a wide-angle view of customer movement, employee activity, weapon handling interactions, and behavior near secured weapon display cases.

- (frame 900) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into a glass display case inside a retail store, appearing to remove merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing while positioned behind the counter area. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1020) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively reaching into and clearing out the contents of a glass display case inside a retail store. Both individuals are leaning over the counter, appearing to remove merchandise from the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1080) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over a display counter in a gun store, with one person appearing to reach into the case while the other stands nearby. The store displays numerous firearms on the walls and in glass cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes.
- (frame 1140) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively removing firearms from a wall display in a store, with one person holding a large bag. Flags — gun: yes; reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1200) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively removing firearms from a wall display in what appears to be a burglary. One person is pulling items from the wall while the other is placing them into a large bag. Weapon note: Multiple firearms are visible being removed from the wall display.. Flags — gun: yes; reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):

======================================================
VLM frame 1440 (window 1320-1440)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio

======================================================
VLM frame 1620 (window 1500-1620)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)
Description: Licensed firearm and tactical equipment store located in Houston, Texas, USA. The business sells legal firearms, ammunition, tactical accessories, protective equipment, and hunting gear. The store includes secured weapon display areas, locked glass showcases, a cashier counter, and restricted staff-only storage areas containing additional inventory.

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.
Location: 1450 Westheimer Rd, Houston, TX, USA, Houston, Texas
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: high
Allowed behaviors: Employees handling firearms, Customers viewing weapons with employee supervision, Cashier processing firearm purchases, Customers discussing products with staff
Forbidden behaviors: Pointing firearms at people, Threatening staff or customers with a weapon, Attempting armed robbery, Aggressive behavior involving weapons, Unauthorized access behind the cashier area, Attempting to steal firearms or ammunition, Rapid escape after suspicious activity, Concealing identity with balaclava or ski mask, Loading firearms inside the store without authorization, Taking weapons from display areas without employee permissio
Surveillance cameras (1) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Main Store Security Camera: The camera monitors the cashier desk, the main entrance and exit door, the firearm display showcases, and the main shopping area inside the store. It provides a wide-angle view of customer movement, employee activity, weapon handling interactions, and behavior near secured weapon display cases.

INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

======================================================
VLM frame 1920 (window 1800-1920)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Lone Star Tactical Arms (gun_store)

A security camera monitors the cashier desk, the firearm display showcases, the main shopping area, and the main entrance/exit door. The surveillance system is designed to detect suspicious or criminal behavior such as armed robbery attempts, threatening behavior, weapon misuse inside the store, unauthorized access to restricted areas, physical aggression, rapid escape attempts, suspicious concealment of identity, and attempts to steal firearms or ammunition.

Weapons being visible, handled by employees, or legally presented to customers are considered normal business activity. The system should only flag dangerous or criminal behavior involving weapons, threats, intimidation, robbery, or unsafe handling.

======================================================
VLM frame 2160 (window 2040-2160)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes
